Text:"As medical AI is increasingly used for patient consultation and clinical decision support, identifying harmful, non-compliant, or ethically problematic responses is essential for responsible deployment. Assessing these risks requires checking clinical claims against relevant evidence, verifying calculations, and applying medical requirements under their intended conditions. We introduce LingSante MedGuard, capability-enhanced guardrail that integrates supervision from medical guardrail teacher model and six specialized medical capability experts into unified model. The guardrail teacher supervises risk decisions and explanations across medical safety, compliance, and ethics, while the capability experts support medical rule understanding, numerical reasoning, multimodal hallucination detection, agentic safety verification, risk localization, and multimodal entity understanding. In particular, rule understanding and agentic verification support evidencegrounded assessment through the application of explicit requirements and the acquisition and appraisal of external medical evidence. We consolidate these sources of supervision through offline supervised fine-tuning on teacher-generated demonstrations, followed by multi-teacher on-policy distillation using feedback on student-generated responses. We further introduce LingSante MedGuardBench, benchmark comprising 11 risk-detection subsets spanning medical safety, compliance, and ethics. At 4B, 9B, and 27B scales, LingSante MedGuard achieves mean benchmark F1 scores of 82.3%, 82.4%, and 87.4%, respectively. The 4B model also achieves 73.9% mean accuracy across MedHallu and MedHALT-NOTA under retrieval-enabled evaluation. Additional evaluations of the verification components show improvements in evidence-supported consistency, source traceability, and rule-guided rationale quality across model scales. These results support the integration of specialized medical capabilities into unified guardrail for medical risk assessme"

Text:"As medical AI is increasingly used for patient consultation and clinical decision support, identifying harmful, non-compliant, or ethically problematic responses is essential for responsible deployment. Assessing these risks requires checking clinical claims against relevant evidence, verifying calculations, and applying medical requirements under their intended conditions. We introduce LingSante MedGuard, capability-enhanced guardrail that integrates supervision from medical guardrail teacher model and six specialized medical capability experts into unified model. The guardrail teacher supervises risk decisions and explanations across medical safety, compliance, and ethics, while the capability experts support medical rule understanding, numerical reasoning, multimodal hallucination detection, agentic safety verification, risk localization, and multimodal entity understanding. In particular, rule understanding and agentic verification support evidencegrounded assessment through the application of explicit requirements and the acquisition and appraisal of external medical evidence. We consolidate these sources of supervision through offline supervised fine-tuning on teacher-generated demonstrations, followed by multi-teacher on-policy distillation using feedback on student-generated responses. We further introduce LingSante MedGuardBench, benchmark comprising 11 risk-detection subsets spanning medical safety, compliance, and ethics. At 4B, 9B, and 27B scales, LingSante MedGuard achieves mean benchmark F1 scores of 82.3%, 82.4%, and 87.4%, respectively. The 4B model also achieves 73.9% mean accuracy across MedHallu and MedHALT-NOTA under retrieval-enabled evaluation. Additional evaluations of the verification components show improvements in evidence-supported consistency, source traceability, and rule-guided rationale quality across model scales. These results support the integration of specialized medical capabilities into unified guardrail for medical risk assessment and evidence-based medical safety verification. Figure 1: Overall averages all 11 text and multimodal benchmark scores; Overall-Text averages only the eight text benchmark scores. Lower panels report mean F1 on shared text tasks: safety (HB/MSB/Rx-I/Rx-C), compliance (HB/MSB), and ethics (HB/MSB). HB: HealthBench; MSB: MedSafetyBench; Rx-I/Rx-C: medication-interaction/contraindication tasks.Recent advances in medical language and multimodal models have expanded AI support for patient consultation, clinical decision support, and medical document interpretation (Jiang et al., 2025; McDuff et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2026a). Models such as MedGemma demonstrate the value of adapting general-purpose models to medical knowledge and data (Sellergren et al., 2025). As these systems enter medical workflows, evaluating their outputs requires attention to both clinical correctness and the conditions governing their use. Responses may contain inappropriate recommendations, violate requirements governing medical practice, or compromise patient rights and professional conduct (Draelos et al., 2026; Yang et al., 2026b). These concerns motivate dedicated medical guardrails that identify risks and explain their judgments in context (Gangavarapu, 2024; Hakim et al., 2024). Existing guardrail research provides foundation for this goal. General-purpose guardrails offer established approaches to broad content-safety classification (Han et al., 2024a; Inan et al., 2023; Team, 2026; Wu et al., 2024; Zhao et al., 2025a), but medical risks often involve specialized knowledge, patient context, and specific medical requirements that are difficult to assess using general content-safety criteria alone. Researchers have therefore developed guardrails for specific medical settings, including diagnostic workflows (Jain et al., 2025), clinical risk detection in telemedicine (Shi et al., 2026), and medical error detection, localization, and correction (Ma et al., 2026a). By examining clinical claims, identifying potential errors, and verifying relevant evidence, these approaches help detect medical risks that general safety classification may overlook. Building on this work, developing unified guardrail for practical medical workflows requires addressing two further issues: risk coverage and the capabilities needed to support risk judgments. First, existing approaches primarily identify risks in specific settings or tasks, but risks in real medical interactions extend beyond the correctness of clinical content to include the boundaries of systems professional role and the protection of patient information and rights (Han et al., 2024b; Yang et al., 2024b). These requirements call for corresponding risk checks: detecting harmful dosage errors concerns medical safety; determining whether system exceeds its authorized professional role concerns compliance; and checking whether response discloses patient identity and medical conditions to unauthorized parties concerns privacy protection and medical ethics. These risks may coexist in one response but require different assessment criteria: clinical correctness establishes neither compliance with professional responsibilities nor adequate protection of patient privacy and rights. Detecting individual problems therefore cannot fully address the multiple risks within medical interaction. common assessment framework must preserve domain-specific criteria. Building on existing frameworks for medical ethics, patient safety, and trustworthiness assessment (Bian et al., 2025; Ding et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2024b), we consolidate assessment requirements across medical safety, compliance, and ethics. This enables unified guardrail to identify risks in the same response and patient context using domain-specific criteria, while drawing on shared medical knowledge to support cross-domain judgments. Second, cross-domain risk assessment also requires specialized medical capabilities that enable guardrails to identify specific risks in responses and verify the basis for their judgments. For responses generated from medical visual inputs, risks may arise from misinterpreting the source information or making medical claims unsupported by the image. Figure 2 illustrates these two problems. The first case concerns an incorrect interpretation of laboratory results: the reported lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) value of 204.50 U/L is incorrectly attributed to creatine kinase (CK), whose actual reported value is 39.00 U/L. Text:"KMed-31B Technical ReportWe present KMed-31B, medical large language model post-trained from an openweight model for on-premises deployment with limited hardware. Post-training model for diverse medical tasks remains difficult, because improving one kind of task can lower performance on another. We attribute this to differences between tasks in expected output and available feedback. Medical question answering and diagnosis prediction have reference answers, whereas clinical inquiry has no single correct response and is judged with rubrics. We therefore divide medical training tasks into four categories by these two properties, train one specialist per category with reward that its feedback supports, and integrate the specialists into single student with Multi-teacher On-policy Delta Distillation (MOPD2). For clinical inquiry, we build Synthetic Health Dialogue, simulated corpus of consultations with lay users and clinicians whose patient cases span common clinical presentations in primary care. Relative to its base model, KMed-31B raises HealthBench Hard from 22.95 to 41.87 and HealthBench Professional from 37.92 to 50.20 in non-thinking mode, and from 23.80 to 51.48 and from 41.97 to 59.18 in thinking mode, while its scores on four multiple-choice medical benchmarks are maintained or slightly improved. KMed-31B has the highest unadjusted HealthBench Hard score among the compared models, including proprietary ones, and scores higher on HealthBench Professional than every open-weight model we compare, including models with more than ten times as many total parameters. Applied to another base model, Qwen3.8-27B, the same training recipe also raises both HealthBench scores. We describe the training recipe, data construction, and settings in detail so that institutions requiring on-premises deployment can develop medical models from open-weight foundations.Frontier-level large language models have demonstrated strong performance on medical question answering and health conversations [1, 2]. In real clinical settings, however, services are often not allowed to send patient data outside the institution and must therefore run models on premises with limited hardware resources. This requirement has motivated efforts to develop relatively small open-weight medical models [3, 4]. Despite their small size, these models must still answer knowledge-intensive medical questions, reason about clinical cases, and handle consultations with lay users and clinicians. Recent open-weight models with around 30B total parameters match much larger models on individual medical benchmarks, but their strengths are uneven across multiple-choice medical questions and health conversations. Gemma-4-31B-IT is on par with GPT-5.6-sol [5] on MedXpertQA Text but falls far behind it on HealthBench Hard and HealthBench Professional, whereas Muse-Glimmer-30B [6] approaches Qwen3.5-397B [7] on both HealthBench benchmarks while its MedXpertQA Text score falls well below that of Gemma-4-31B-IT (Section 4.2). Improving one of these capabilities through post-training can come at the expense of the other. ConRub-Med reports that rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) on Qwen3-32B raises HealthPreprint. Figure 1: Medical performance vs. price in thinking and non-thinking modes. Medical performance is the mean of the scores on HealthBench Hard, HealthBench Professional, MedXpertQA Text, JAMA Clinical Challenge, NEJM AI QA, and MedQA-USMLE. Blended prices use 3:1 input-to-output token ratio. We plot Gemma-4-31B-IT at $0.25 per million tokens and assign the same price to KMed-31B. For the other models, we use the list prices reported by Artificial Analysis [8] (first-party API prices when available, otherwise the median across providers). Frontier curves are interpolated between Pareto-optimal points, and the arrow shows the thinking-mode increase from Gemma-4-31BIT to KMed-31B. Bench Hard from 9.46 to 33.17 while lowering MedXpertQA Text from 22.41 to 17.55 [9]. In an Apertus-70B data ablation of Fully Open Meditron, adding synthetic clinical vignettes to the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) mixture improves open-ended clinical preference but lowers MedQA accuracy by 1.33 points [10]. We observe similar pattern when fine-tuning Gemma-4-31B-IT on consultation dialogues, which improves HealthBench performance but lowers MedXpertQA Text and JAMA Clinical Challenge accuracy even with medical multiple-choice examples mixed in (Section 5.1). The recurrence of this trade-off across base models and across both SFT and RL leads us to focus on how the tasks are posed and supervised rather than on particular model or training method. Medical question answering has reference answers against which response can be checked, whereas clinical inquiry covers open-ended requests from lay users and clinicians that have no single correct response. The quality of clinical response depends on several aspects at once, such as its accuracy, its completeness, and its appropriateness to the users context, and its evaluation therefore requires rubrics that specify criteria for each aspect. Medical question answering and clinical inquiry thus differ in both expected output and available feedback. In this sense, they constitute distinct domains, as mathematical reasoning and open-ended chat do in general-purpose post-training. Such domains tend to interfere when trained together in single run [11, 12], and each therefore calls for its own training procedure. Prior medical post-training has addressed such interference by refining the training data mixture or by integrating separately trained specialists [13, 14]; we make this distinction explicit and use it to decide which specialists to train and how to train each one. We develop KMed-31B from Gemma-4-31B-IT through post-training recipe that groups medical tasks by expected output and available training feedback and assigns one specialist to each category (Section 2.1). Open-ended question answering, multiple-choice question answering, and diagnosis prediction provide reference answers, so their specialists are trained through RL with answer-matching rewards. Text:"November 24, 2026 Qwen-Image-HD: Autoregressive Diffusion for High Definition Image Generation Zihao Liu, Zekai Zhang, Shengming Yin, Zikai Zhou, Donghao Zhou, Chenyang Zhu, Lihan Jiang, Ningyuan Tang, Bingxuan Zhao, Yanran Zhang, Yan Shu, Zhening Liu, Jiahao Li, Jie Zhang, Kaiyuan Gao, Kun Yan, Qihang Ai, Rui Song, Shuo Cao, Xiao Xu, Xiaoyue Chen, Yuxiang Chen, Zhendong Wang, Zhouyang Li, Bo Zheng, Jun Zhou, Xiu Li, Chenfei WuFine-tuning generator on complete high-resolution canvas requires long image sequences and substantial computation, yet local regions can still exhibit unnatural repetitive stripes when training resources are limited. We introduce Qwen-Image-HD, which adapts Qwen-Image 2.0 to high-resolution synthesis through local training and blockwise autoregressive generation. Each prediction covers target tile at 1K scale, conditioned on high-resolution upper and left neighbors and compressed historical thumbnail. These conditions provide adjacent fine-scale structures and an overview of the scene generated so far. To integrate these complementary cues, we map both forms of history and the target to shared canvas coordinate system to express their spatial relationships and scale differences. On Qwen-Image-Bench at 4K, Qwen-ImageHD achieves stronger overall evaluation performance than direct 4K generation with FLUX-3-Image-Klein. Compared with full-image 4K supervised fine-tuning of the same backbone, it achieves better generation quality with lower training compute and higher inference efficiency, while exhibiting fewer unnatural repetitive stripes in originalresolution comparisons. We further introduce Wavefront-AutoRegressive inference schedule that generates tiles on the same anti-diagonal concurrently across GPUs to reduce single-image latency. Figure 1: Images generated by Qwen-Image-HD. Left: multiple 4K examples. Right: single 16K image with enlarged details. Corresponding author.High-definition text-to-image generation requires coherent global structure and fine local detail. Recent work (Zhang et al., 2025a; Ye et al., 2026) improves 4K synthesis by adapting pretrained generators through post-training with specialized data, representations, and objectives. However, training on complete high-resolution images requires processing long image sequences at every update. As the canvas grows, this increases the cost of adapting the generator, limiting the number of updates possible within given budget. Coherent composition alone also does not ensure that individual regions contain convincing detail. This motivates central question: how can pretrained generator acquire high-definition synthesis capabilities efficiently while preserving both scene structure and local detail? Blockwise prediction shortens the target sequence, but local targets still need context to form coherent image. NUWA-Infinity (Liang et al., 2022) combines patchand token-level autoregression, propagating historical information through cached neighborhood features. ACDiT (Hu et al., 2024) combines blockwise diffusion with skip-causal attention and KV caching, while MADFormer (Chen et al., 2025a) separates autoregressive conditioning from iterative diffusion refinement across model layers. Although both reuse historical computations, their retained context grows with the preceding sequence. Reducing target size therefore does not by itself bound the cost of conditioning on developing canvas. We seek history representation that supplies nearby detail and an explicit broader overview without retaining every preceding high-resolution token. We introduce Qwen-Image-HD, which adapts Qwen-Image 2.0 (Zhao et al., 2026) through 1K tile prediction with bounded, spatially organized history to generate high-definition images (Figure 1). fixed-size historical thumbnail summarizes completed canvas regions, while boundary regions of the upper and left neighbors retain fine structures lost during downsampling. Unfilled regions remain white, so the thumbnail represents only available history during both training and generation. Its token count stays fixed as more canvas regions are completed, providing an expanding scene overview without accumulating high-resolution history. These conditions serve complementary roles: the thumbnail conveys broader scene organization, and the neighbors constrain continuity with adjacent regions. Their different resolutions and spatial extents, however, require the model to distinguish where each condition belongs. We therefore map the thumbnail, neighbors, and target to shared canvas coordinates that preserve their relative positions and scales. This dual-scale representation connects local training targets to the larger scene within bounded context-token budget. At inference, Serial-AutoRegressive sampling uses the same context structure to assemble local predictions into complete image, updating history after each tile. Sequential sampling, however, increases single-image waiting time even when each prediction is inexpensive. We introduce Wavefront-AutoRegressive inference to generate tiles on the same anti-diagonal concurrently while preserving upper/left dependencies. Each round uses shared history, which is updated when the round completes. Our implementation combines concurrent tile generation with within-tile parallelism and dynamic GPU allocation to accelerate rounds with fewer active tiles. The schedule applies directly to the Serial-trained model without additional training, reducing latency through parallel execution with latencyquality trade-off relative to Serial. We evaluate adaptation efficiency and generation quality at 4K against training-free resolution extrapolation and training-based high-resolution methods. controlled comparison with full-image supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of the same backbone shows that Qwen-Image-HD reaches higher Q-Judger Overall (Li et al., 2026) with lower cumulative training FLOPs under the evaluated budgets and configurations. Original-resolution comparisons and Narrowband Spectral Excess (NSE) on Nature-384 indicate fewer unnatural repetitive stripes than SFT. Controlled comparisons with alternative compact history designs also support the thumbnail representation within our framework. With four GPUs, Wavefront further reduces latency relative to Serial and full-image SFT. Our main contributions are: Dual-scale history and alignment. We propose spatially aligned, dual-scale history representation for local adaptation of pretrained imag"

Text:"ICLWorld: Benchmarking In-Context Learning of Agents from Digital to Physical WorldsPei-lin LiSunqi Fan Deyao Hong Huan-ang Gao Meng-Hao Guo Shuojin YangProject page: iclworld.github.io Code: github.com/ICLWorld/ICLWorld Dataset: huggingface.co/datasets/Aoraku/ICLWorld Corresponding author Figure 1. Overview of ICLWorld. Each task comes in three rule versions that require different behavior for the same goal, and the rule is given as text, as video, or not at all. The same agent acts in the digital and the physical world through one harness, UIverse, and is evaluated with rule-specific verifiers, rule questions, and no-rule control across three difficulty levels and three generalization levels. Right: the 160 tasks by domain and rule type, and the success rates of six frontier agents with text rules.In-context learning (ICL) lets agents acquire new rules from context and adapt to user-specific requirements at inference time. The same frontier agents now operate both computers and robots, yet their ICL remains insufficiently assessed: existing benchmarks are largely offline, rely on text or static images, and risk training-data contamination, while robot evaluations are limited to method-level ablations. We present ICLWorld, an online benchmark that evaluates the ICL of agents from digital to physical worlds, covering computer use and robot use, together with UIverse, unified harness that turns every environment into user interface. We build 30 environments, record 630 demonstration videos (10+ hours), and design 160 tasks with customized, often counter-intuitive rules that cannot be satisfied without learning from the context. Each task comes with three rule versions, given as text or video, and we test generalization at three levels, from replaying the demonstration to new instances and unseen environments. Across six frontier models, the strongest agents, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, succeed on 82% and 76% of computer-use runs but only 27% 1 and 26% of robot-use runs, and no-rule control reduces success to at most 8%. GPT-6 Astra succeeds under all three rule versions on only 38% of computer-use tasks, half its rate under single rule, and answers 98% of robot rule questions correctly yet completes only 20% of these tasks. Frontier agents learn rules comparably well from video and from text and transfer them across environments, whereas weaker agents rely on text and lose most of their video-based success once the demonstrated situation is resampled. These findings reveal substantial gaps between the digital and the physical world and between identifying rule and acting on it. 1. Introduction In-context learning (ICL), the ability to acquire new rules, knowledge, and procedures from the input without any parameter update [8, 21], is what allows general-purpose model to follow the conventions of particular user, team, or workplace rather than its pre-trained priors. This ability matters most for agents that act. Today, the same frontier models operate computers as computer-use agents (CUAs) and, increasingly, robots as robot-use agents (RUAs) [13, 64]. In both settings, users teach task in the most natural way: they show demonstration or state rule, and expect the agent to apply it to new data and in new surroundings. Measuring this ability faithfully is difficult, and existing ICL benchmarks fall short in four ways. First, most of them present the context as text or static images and evaluate offline, whereas acting agents learn from videos and must carry out what they learned in interactive environments. Second, their rules and data are often drawn from public sources and may have entered pre-training. Third, as models grow stronger, many tasks become solvable without reading the context at all [9], so success no longer reflects learning. Fourth, with single rule per task, they cannot tell whether an agent follows the given rule or its own prior, nor whether it can transfer the rule beyond the demonstrated situation. The physical world is even less understood. Recent RUAs increasingly rely on frontier models to learn from in-context demonstrations, yet this ability has been examined only within individual systems, with conflicting conclusions [15, 32, 53, 89]. Moreover, although computer use and robot use share the same perceiveact loop, no benchmark asks whether rule-learning ability that works on screens carries over to robots when the agent and its interface are held fixed. We present ICLWORLD  (Fig. 1)  , benchmark that evaluates the in-context rule learning of agents from the digital to the physical world. ICLWORLD contains 160 tasks in two domains: computer use on web and OS platforms, and tabletop manipulation, in which dual-arm robot learns from real-world human demonstrations and acts in physics simulation. Each task asks the agent to reach goal under customized, often counter-intuitive rule that is given only in the context, either as text description or as video demonstration. ICLWORLD evaluates in-context learning end to end, from inferring the rule to completing the task under it. ICLWORLD is built on four principles: Leakage-free. We build all 30 environments and record all 630 demonstration videos (10+ hours) ourselves, around customized rules rather than public material. Context-dependent. Without the rule, task can only be completed by chance, and each task comes in three rule versions that demand different behavior in the same situation. Challenging. Tasks range from short rule applications to long-horizon workflows and fine manipulation, on par with or harder than current frontier benchmarks. Diagnostic. We test whether learned rules generalize to new instances and unseen environments, and probe the gap between rule recognition and task completion. All agents interact through UIVERSE, unified harness that presents web applications, desktops, and robot environments through the same kind of graphical interface. This removes variations from domain-specific interfaces and enables consistent evaluation across both worlds. We evaluate six frontier models in their native agent CLIs. The strongest, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, succeed on 82% and 76% of computer-use runs but only 27% and 26% of robot-use runs, and the other four models trail far behind in both domains. Without the rule, success drops to at most 8%, confirming that the tasks require learning from context. GPT-6 Astra succeeds under all three rule versions on only 38% of computer-use tasks, ha"

Text:"Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation: Pixel as Code for Agentic Visual Creation 2026-11-Visual creation demands expressive imagery together with precise control over text, layout, and structure. We introduce Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation, an agentic framework that connects these capabilities through Pixel as Code. Raster images are represented by learned discrete visual tokens, while structured artifacts use explicit programs. shared generation-and-rendering interface connects these representations to visual feedback across images, vector graphics, webpages, and documents. Nemotron-native harness coordinates planning and raster candidate selection, with targeted repair for editable structured and hybrid artifacts. To support this interface, we develop Nemotron-Labs-Image-Tokenizer, the autoregressive raster generator Nemotron-Labs-Image-30B-A3B, and the structured-code model family Nemotron-Labs-VisionCode. On approximately 178K images spanning six visual domains, our tokenizer improves reconstruction PSNR from 26.08 to 29.57 dB over Emu3.5 IBQ. It also provides 7.13 faster encoding and 10.93 faster decoding at batch size one on A100. In standalone evaluation, one Nemotron-Labs-VisionCode variant surpasses Kimi K3.1 Pro on the aggregate across Chart2Code, MMSVG, Design2Code, and Image2Struct. At the system level, the Nemotron-native configuration raises GenEval2 Soft-TIFA GM from 31.71 to 52.56 using the same raster generator. With our best setting, Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation achieves performance competitive with GPT Image 2 and Nano Banana Pro across most benchmarks with available comparisons. https://huggingface.co/nvidia/Nemotron-Labs-Image-30B-A3B (a) Raster images (b) Editable structured artifacts Visual tokens 𝑧1, 𝑧2, . . . , 𝑧𝑇 Tokenizer decoder Pixels Explicit programs SVG / HTML / LaTeX Renderers and compilers Editable artifacts Appearance Visual text Web interfaces Charts and diagrams Shared interface: Generate representations Render visual outputs Inspect results Figure 1: Pixel as Code at glance. Learned visual tokens and explicit programs provide complementary representations for raster images and editable structured artifacts. Their renderers connect both representations to visual outputs that agents can inspect. Hybrid artifacts combine raster assets with structured layouts. Representations are schematic; thumbnails show representative system outputs. Figure 5 details the route-specific generation, selection, and repair workflows. 2026 NVIDIA. All rights reserved. Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation: Pixel as Code for Agentic Visual Creation Figure 2: Raster image generation examples from Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation. The examples span illustrated infographics, text-rich compositions, portraits, stylized typography, and product imagery, illustrating diverse visual styles and content requirements. 2 Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation: Pixel as Code for Agentic Visual Creation Figure 3: Raster image generation examples from Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation, spanning natural landscapes, wildlife, and portraiture. The examples showcase fine visual detail, diverse lighting conditions, and range of textures and compositions. 3 Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation: Pixel as Code for Agentic Visual Creation Figure 4: Editable structured artifacts created by Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation, including webpages, mobile interfaces, vector icon systems, presentation slides, scientific figures, and charts. The framework combines generated visual assets with explicit representations to compose text, graphics, and layouts that can be rendered and further edited. 4 Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation: Pixel as Code for Agentic Visual Creation 1. Introduction Visual creation spans photographs, illustrations, charts, webpages, posters, scientific figures, and slides. single request may demand expressive imagery, exact wording, factual accuracy, spatial relationships, and an editable deliverable. Meeting these needs calls for both expressive synthesis and explicit control over content and structure, coordinated through representation, rendering, and visual inspection. Recent work provides complementary foundations. Edify Image (NVIDIA et al., 2024), Sana (Xie et al., 2024), and PixelDiT2 (Yu et al., 2026) explore representations and architectures for image synthesis, while Cosmos 3 (NVIDIA, 2026a) integrates multimodal understanding and generation. Agentic image creation (Li et al., 2026b) and scene-generation systems (Ling et al., 2026; Xia et al., 2026) investigate planning, tool use, and visual assessment. These directions motivate common interface for coordinating learned visual generation and explicit artifact structure. We introduce Pixel as Code, shared interface in which generators predict discrete representations and renderers produce visual outputs for inspection (Figure 1). Learned visual tokens capture raster appearance and are decoded into pixels. Explicit programsincluding SVG, HTML/CSS, plotting code, and document markupexpose content, geometry, and layout for direct editing through their respective rendering backends. Text:"LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference Anh Dao #World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, compact WAM that recurrently applies shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using 7.4 less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracylatency operating points from single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks. (cid:128) Project page Code (cid:243) Models # Correspondence anh.dao@ucf.edu https://anhdao69.github.io/LoopedWAM https://github.com/anhdao69/LoopedWAM https://huggingface.co/anhdao69/LoopedWAM Figure 1. LoopedWAM is small, fast, and elastic. (a) Each expert stores 12 blocks and loops its shared 6-block core times. One checkpoint thus reaches the 30-layer depth of its dense donor with 2.4 fewer parameters, and the depth is chosen at test time. (b) RoboTwin 2.0 success against parameters, as reported by each paper. At 0.58B, LoopedWAM (91.9%) is the best model below 2B and on par with the 6B Fast-WAM (91.8%). (c) LIBERO success against latency on one H100 for each setting of action loops Ka and sampling steps of LoopedWAM-E. One checkpoint spans 24.786.2 ms at 95.296.2% success, and at (2, 2) it is 3.9 faster than Fast-WAM. 1 LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference Dao et al.A practical robot policy must satisfy two competing demands: producing accurate actions from visual observations and language instructions, while operating within the GPU memory and latency constraints of closed-loop control. Recent world action models (WAMs) have made remarkable progress on the former by leveraging pretrained video diffusion models to learn robotic actions, often through jointly training video and action experts [5, 29, 33, 60, 63]. Drawing on physical priors from web-scale video pretraining, WAMs have achieved competitive performance with vision-language-action models (VLAs) on benchmarks such as LIBERO [39], RoboTwin 2.0 [12], and RoboCasa [45], often without robot pretraining. However, these gains come at cost that conflicts with the latter demand. Most current WAMs carry 2B to 14B parameters and repeatedly execute deep action expert across multiple denoising steps, whereas closed-loop control calls for small memory footprint, low latency, and the flexibility to trade accuracy for speed without retraining. Two natural approaches address different aspects of this challenge, but neither is sufficient on its own. Sharing weights across depth reduces the number of stored parameters without reducing the sequential computation required at fixed execution depth. Conversely, reducing the number of sampling steps lowers latency but may degrade action quality when the policy is not trained for fewer steps. practical compact WAM must therefore answer two questions: how much capacity must it store, and how much computation must it execute to produce useful actions? Answering these questions starts from where WAM spends its computation. Fast-WAM [63] showed that WAM need not generate future video at test time: the benefit of video modeling comes from joint training, allowing the video expert to encode the current observation once while the action expert generates an action chunk over sampling steps. query therefore requires one video-expert pass and action-expert passes. At batch size one, for comparable block architectures, latency is primarily determined by the number of sequentially executed transformer blocks rather than parameter count alone (Section 4.3, Figure 3c). These observations suggest two complementary opportunities: reducing parameter storage through weight sharing and reducing execution cost by allocating computation differently between the two experts. ① For the first question, we turn to looped, or weight-shared, transformers [2, 15, 20, 22], which reach the depth of deep network with the parameters of shallow one by repeatedly applying shared group of blocks. We bring this idea to world action models with LoopedWAM (Figure 1a). Each expert stores 12 blocks organized into 3-block prelude, 6-block shared core, and 3-block coda. Repeating the shared core four times recovers the 30-layer depth of its Wan2.1 donor [53]. The resulting policy has 0.58B parameters, 2.4 fewer than an equally deep dense model built from the same donor. Importantly, this makes execution depth potential test-time choice without changing the stored parameters. ② For the second question, the asymmetric inference costs of the two experts suggest that their execution depths can be controlled independently. Given this asymmetry, LoopedWAM allows the two experts to use different loop counts, with late alignment enabling even single action loop to access the deepest visual features. However, adjustable execution depth does not guarantee reliable inference at reduced depth: without elastic training, reducing the action expert to single loop drops LIBERO success to 12.3% under our ablation training schedule (Figure 3a). To address this, we introduce elastic self-distillation, which trains single checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, to operate across action-loop counts Ka {1, 2, 4} and sampling steps {2, 5, 10}, while keeping the video expert at full depth. By matching frozen full-depth teacher starting from the students own intermediate states, the student learns to correct errors introduce"

Text:"Distill to Loop: Turning Pretrained Diffusion Transformers into Looped Models with Adjustable DepthShengwei Anwinterccccc@gmail.com swan@vt.edu Code Models (cid:209) Project page Figure 1. Samples of the Dooop student distilled from Z-Image.Looped transformers reuse one block of shared weights along the depth of the network. Recent looped diffusion transformers are trained from scratch, which repeats the full pretraining cost although strong pretrained nonlooped diffusion transformers exist. We propose Distill to Loop (Dooop), which distills pretrained non-looped difCorresponding author. fusion transformer into looped student whose blocks are initialized from the teachers. We cast distillation as fixedpoint iteration toward the teacher, in which the output after every loop is trained toward the teacher, and each loop is one step of an ordinary differential equation in depth. As result, one model runs any number of loops at test time without retraining, and the higher-order ODE solvers of diffusion model sampling can be applied along the depth dimension for better results. Meanwhile, because looped model produces an output after every loop, we also construct 1 loop guidance between the outputs of different loop counts, which improves the generated samples at little extra compute. On SD3-medium, looped student with about 37% of the teachers parameters matches the teacher on average over GenEval, ImageReward, PickScore and HPSv2.1 with two to four loops, and with two loops it uses about 58% of the teachers compute. And on four-step SD3-medium, loop guidance raises the average of the four metrics from 88.2 to 99.2. 1. Introduction Looped transformers, which apply block of shared weights repeatedly along the depth of the network, have recently gained traction in language modeling [7, 14, 53]. looped model can spend more computation at inference time by running more iterations. The accuracy of recurrentdepth language models improves as the number of iterations grows at test time [14], and the looped model Ouro [53] approaches the quality of larger dense models. Sharing weights across iterations reduces the parameter count and raises throughput [2, 3], and pretrained non-looped language models can be converted into looped ones [2, 33, 36]. Diffusion models are among the generative models most limited by inference time, and video diffusion models most of all, because generating one video runs large backbone, such as the 14B-parameter Wan [43], for many denoising steps. Step distillation reduces the number of steps, for example through progressive distillation [38], consistency models [40], distribution matching distillation [47, 48], and its video extension CausVid [49]. Each remaining step still runs the full backbone, so inference stays long. Looped transformers have recently appeared in visual generation as well. Looped-DiT [6], ELT [17], and LiFT [8] show that small set of shared blocks can replace deep stack of distinct blocks. In these models, running fewer loops gives faster sample. But all of these looped diffusion models are trained from scratch on the full dataset. Obtaining looped model repeats the full pretraining cost, although strong pretrained non-looped diffusion transformers already exist. This leads us to two questions. The first question asks whether looped model can be obtained faster by distilling an existing pretrained model. And the second question concerns how many loops looped model needs. looped model is fixed-point iteration, so good one should reach its fixed point within few loops. Some existing looped diffusion transformers [8, 17] reach the quality of non-looped model only at large depth, at their full loop count or with more loops than they were trained with. Other models [6] supervise every loop with the final objective, so their early loops converge sooner, but their quality drops once they run more loops than they were trained with. Methods that adapt depth at test time, such as choosing sampling steps per prompt [51], skipping calls or layers [4, 42, 46], routing to pruned experts [13], or exiting loop early [6], only remove computation. We call this question adjustable depth. It asks whether looped model obtained by distillation can converge within few loops, so that small loop count already matches the teacher, while it keeps the ability to extend its depth, with the loop count and the depth solver set at inference without retraining. Building on these two questions, we propose Distill to Loop (Dooop). Dooop takes pretrained non-looped diffusion transformer, keeps few leading and trailing blocks, and replaces the middle stack by small set of shared blocks that are looped. Every block of the student is initialized from teacher block, so training starts from the teachers weights. We cast distillation as fixed-point iteration in which the student moves toward the teacher loop by loop, so during training the teacher velocity is the fixed point. The target of the output after every loop is the result of one iteration from the previous loops output toward the teacher. Every loop is therefore pulled toward the teacher, each loop is tied to the loop before it, and the last loop reaches the teacher. This allows small loop count to already land close to the teacher. In the forward pass, we model the students loops as an ordinary differential equation in depth over the depth interval from 0 to 1. One step of this equation is relaxed fixed-point step whose step size is set by continuous depth coordinate. Every loop count therefore covers the same depth interval, so one model runs any number of loops at inference without retraining. At the same time, because the loops are steps of this equation, we can also apply here the higher-order solvers of diffusion model sampling [19, 29, 30] to obtain more accurate iteration. As for the training states, Dooop trains not only on noisy data states but also on on-policy states, which sampler that combines the teacher and the current student visits, so that training covers the states the student meets when it samples. For sampling, we design loop guidance based on the looped structure, which uses the output of an earlier loop in the same forward pass as the weak model and improves the samples at little extra compute. We evaluate Dooop on class-conditional ImageNet [37] generation with SiT-XL/2 as the teacher and mainly on textto-image generation with SD3-medium. On SD3-medium, our looped student has 0.756B parameters, about"

Text:"Foresight Forcing: Learning Spatial Foresight for Robotic Manipulation Mishuo Han1,3,4,*, Zhixuan Liang2,*,, Zhaoyuan Wan1,4,*, Wendi Chen1,3,4,*, Jiarui Chen3,4,5, Xinkai Wang3,4, Yue Ma6, Yu Zhou1,3,4, Junting Chen7, Yao Mu1, Jun Lv4, Chuan Wen1, Cewu Lu1,3,4, 1 Shanghai Jiao Tong University 2 The University of Hong Kong 3 Shanghai Innovation Institute 4 Noematrix 5 Fudan University 6 The Hong Kong University of Science and Technology 7 National University of Singapore * Equal contribution. Corresponding authors. Robotic manipulation policies can improve action prediction by anticipating how objects move and how their spatial relationships change. World Action Models (WAMs) learn to predict future visual observations, but their training objectives typically leave the underlying 3D geometric transitions implicit. To alleviate this problem, we introduce Foresight Forcing (F 2), WAM that learns spatial transition representation from changes in pretrained 3D feature space. During training, frozen 3D foundation model extracts patch-wise feature differences from aligned current and future observations that provide dense supervision for geometry-aware feature transitions. We distill these targets into spatial transition queries, which are progressively refined from the current observation, language instruction, and proprioceptive state, and use them alongside current visual features to generate actions. At deployment, the policy predicts the queries without future observations or the 3D teacher. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin 2.0 demonstrate strong manipulation performance and improved robustness, including 95.01% average success on RoboTwin 2.0 Full. On six real-world manipulation tasks, 2 achieves mean task score of 85.8%. Website: https://anson2333.github.io/foresight-forcing Figure 1 Foresight Forcing (F 2) at glance. 2 learns spatial transition queries from correspondence-aligned changes in 3D teacher features. During training, frozen VGGT extracts"

Abstract A reinforcement learning method rewards agents for delaying commitment until their inferred posterior over answers is sufficiently narrow, improving evidence-based answering across vision-language benchmarks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash AI agents working on long tasks often commit to an answer before they have enough evidence, a behavior called laziness. In our evaluations, they frequently commit even while their stated belief still names several possible answers. An outcome reward at the end of the episode scores only the final answer, so it cannot teach the agent when to commit, because a lucky guess receives the same reward as an answer backed by evidence. During training, however, the simulator can compute the exact posterior over the answer at every step and tell whether the evidence is already enough. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Beliefs (RLVB), which rewards the agent for taking further steps while this posterior still allows several answers and for committing once the evidence is sufficient. The reward needs no judge, critic or reward model, and it never reads the belief line the agent writes before each action. We also introduce Pro Worlds, a benchmark of visual puzzles where the agent views a large world through a small window. We evaluate three vision-language models on three benchmarks, two of which are never seen in training. RLVB improves the base model in all nine settings, by 17.1% on average. Under the outcome reward, GRPO and PPO fall below the base model on average. RLVB also outperforms PPO with a process reward from an LLM judge by 9.3% on average while using 1.6 times fewer GPU-hours. The trained agent answers with sufficient evidence in 51%, 12% and 74% of the episodes on the three benchmarks, against 8%, 0.4% and 36% for the base model. Its accuracy gain therefore comes from answering more often at high levels of evidence. The belief line shows how the agent reaches this evidence: it acts on its own belief. It commits at only 0.1 to 2.2% of the turns at which the evidence is insufficient and its line still names several answers, against 3 to 68% for the base model.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract A reinforcement learning method rewards agents for delaying commitment until their inferred posterior over answers is sufficiently narrow, improving evidence-based answering across vision-language benchmarks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash AI agents working on long tasks often commit to an answer before they have enough evidence, a behavior called laziness. In our evaluations, they frequently commit even while their stated belief still names several possible answers. An outcome reward at the end of the episode scores only the final answer, so it cannot teach the agent when to commit, because a lucky guess receives the same reward as an answer backed by evidence. During training, however, the simulator can compute the exact posterior over the answer at every step and tell whether the evidence is already enough. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Beliefs (RLVB), which rewards the agent for taking further steps while this posterior still allows several answers and for committing once the evidence is sufficient. The reward needs no judge, critic or reward model, and it never reads the belief line the agent writes before each action. We also introduce Pro Worlds, a benchmark of visual puzzles where the agent views a large world through a small window. We evaluate three vision-language models on three benchmarks, two of which are never seen in training. RLVB improves the base model in all nine settings, by 17.1% on average. Under the outcome reward, GRPO and PPO fall below the base model on average. RLVB also outperforms PPO with a process reward from an LLM judge by 9.3% on average while using 1.6 times fewer GPU-hours. The trained agent answers with sufficient evidence in 51%, 12% and 74% of the episodes on the three benchmarks, against 8%, 0.4% and 36% for the base model. Its accuracy gain therefore comes from answering more often at high levels of evidence. The belief line shows how the agent reaches this evidence: it acts on its own belief. It commits at only 0.1 to 2.2% of the turns at which the evidence is insufficient and its line still names several answers, against 3 to 68% for the base model."

Abstract  As medical AI is increasingly used for patient consultation and clinical decision support, identifying harmful, non-compliant, or ethically problematic responses is essential for responsible deployment. Assessing these risks requires checking clinical claims against relevant evidence, verifying calculations, and applying medical requirements under their intended conditions. We introduce LingSante MedGuard, a capability-enhanced guardrail that integrates supervision from a medical guardrail teacher model and six specialized medical capability experts into a unified model. The guardrail teacher supervises risk decisions and explanations across medical safety, compliance, and ethics, while the capability experts support medical rule understanding, numerical reasoning, multimodal hallucination detection, agentic safety verification, risk localization, and multimodal entity understanding. We consolidate these sources of supervision through offline supervised fine-tuning on teacher-generated demonstrations, followed by multi-teacher on-policy distillation using feedback on student-generated responses. We further introduce LingSante MedGuardBench, a benchmark comprising 11 risk-detection subsets spanning medical safety, compliance, and ethics. At 4B, 9B, and 27B scales, LingSante MedGuard achieves mean benchmark F1 scores of 82.3%, 82.4%, and 87.4%, respectively. The 4B model also achieves 73.9% mean accuracy across MedHallu and MedHALT-NOTA under retrieval-enabled evaluation. Additional evaluations of the verification components show improvements in evidence-supported consistency, source traceability, and rule-guided rationale quality across model scales. These results support the integration of specialized medical capabilities into a unified guardrail for medical risk assessment and evidence-based medical safety verification.

Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. 'emoji': emoji that will reflect the theme of an article somehow, only one emoji. 'title': a slogan of a main idea of the article in Russian. Return only JSON and nothing else.

Abstract An online benchmark evaluates in-context learning across computer-use and robot-use agent tasks, revealing large gaps between digital and physical performance and between recognizing rules and acting on them.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash In-context learning (ICL) lets agents acquire new rules from context and adapt to user-specific requirements at inference time. The same frontier agents now operate both computers and robots, yet their ICL remains insufficiently assessed: existing benchmarks are largely offline, rely on text or static images, and risk training-data contamination, while robot evaluations are limited to method-level ablations. We present ICLWorld, an online benchmark that evaluates the ICL of agents from digital to physical worlds, covering computer use and robot use, together with UIverse, a unified harness that turns every environment into a user interface. We build 30 environments, record 630 demonstration videos (10+ hours), and design 160 tasks with customized, often counter-intuitive rules that cannot be satisfied without learning from the context. Each task comes with three rule versions, given as text or video, and we test generalization at three levels, from replaying the demonstration to new instances and unseen environments. Across six frontier models, the strongest agents, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, succeed on 82% and 76% of computer-use runs but only 27% and 26% of robot-use runs, and a no-rule control reduces success to at most 8%. GPT-6 Astra succeeds under all three rule versions on only 38% of computer-use tasks, half its rate under a single rule, and answers 98% of robot rule questions correctly yet completes only 20% of these tasks. Frontier agents learn rules comparably well from video and from text and transfer them across environments, whereas weaker agents rely on text and lose most of their video-based success once the demonstrated situation is resampled. These findings reveal substantial gaps between the digital and the physical world and between identifying a rule and acting on it.

Авторы создали 30 сред, 630 видеодемонстраций и 160 задач с нестандартными правилами, которые нельзя выполнить без обучения по контексту, и проверили обобщение на трех уровнях. Лучшие LLM-агенты решают до 82% задач на компьютере, но лишь около 27% задач с роботом, а без правил успех падает до 8%. Результаты показывают большой разрыв между цифровым и физическим миром и между пониманием правила и умением по нему действовать.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Понять правило не значит действовать: агенты в цифре и в реальном мире"}

Abstract  Reasoning models become long-horizon agents through reinforcement learning or, more cheaply, through fine-tuning on a stronger teacher's trajectories, which contain the teacher's chain-of-thought as well as its actions. The standard fine-tuning approach imitates both, yet the strongest teachers are closed models whose chain-of-thought is hidden behind an API. We show that the chain-of-thought can be left out altogether and that the long-horizon policy can be distilled from the teacher's actions alone. The student is trained on these trajectories with its reasoning block left empty, while a KL loss on a replay buffer of its own generations limits drift from its initial model. Surprisingly, the policy learned from these trajectories with an empty reasoning block carries over to inference with thinking enabled, where the student reasons in its own style while acting like the teacher, an effect we call cross-format transfer. Distilling the actions of Qwen3.6-27B raises the task success of Qwen3-4B on AppWorld from 7% to 18% and that of Qwen3-32B from 12% to 25%. We further introduce CoT-proxies, short summaries of the teacher's reasoning placed after the empty reasoning block, which raise Qwen3-4B further, to 35%. Since neither actions nor CoT-proxies need the hidden chain-of-thought, the recipe applies unchanged to closed teachers. Distilling GPT-5.6 Luna from its visible outputs and the reasoning summaries its API returns raises Qwen3-4B from 7% to 48% on AppWorld and from 3% to 28% on SWE-bench Verified.
[25.11.2026 17:52] Response: {"desc": "Авторы показывают, что для дообучения reasoning-моделей, работающих как long-horizon агенты, можно использовать только действия сильного учителя, без его chain-of-thought, который часто скрыт за API. Ученик обучается на траекториях с пустым блоком рассуждений, а KL-loss на replay buffer ограничивает дрейф от исходной LLM. Выученная политика переносится на инференс с включённым thinking (эффект cross-format transfer), и добавление CoT-proxies, кратких саммари рассуждений учителя, дополнительно повышает качество. Abstract  Autonomous specification engineering requires coding agents to translate functional requirements into executable tests and use the results to discover and validate defects. However, existing benchmarks evaluate coding agents on explicitly specified test generation tasks, commonly providing an issue description, a designated test entry point, or files to inspect, leaving the ability to determine what to test and where to investigate insufficiently evaluated. Relying on such clues bypasses the challenge of mapping requirements to implementations and proactively uncovering unknown defects. We propose SpecEngineeringBench, comprising 100 instances from 78 open-source Python repositories across 16 application categories, with multiple recorded defects in each instance. Two independent tasks evaluate complementary capabilities: generating test suites and discovering defects through execution analysis. The evaluation combines test validity, defect detection, and coverage with localization precision and recall. Across seven frontier coding agents, results reveal that test validity and coverage decouple from defect exposure: agents achieve a 91.47% Head Pass Rate and 80.48% Change-focused Coverage alongside a 26.36% Fail-to-Pass Rate, while localization recall drops from 78.11% at the file level to 51.29% at the function level. Autonomous test generation improves file, function, and hunk localization recall by 4.99, 2.77, and 2.69 percentage points over relying solely on existing repository test suites.

Бенчмарк проверяет две задачи: генерацию тестов по функциональным требованиям и обнаружение дефектов через анализ выполнения без явных подсказок. Результаты показывают, что высокая валидность тестов и покрытие не гарантируют выявление ошибок: Fail-to-Pass Rate составляет лишь 26.36%, а полнота локализации падает с 78.11% на уровне файлов до 51.29% на уровне функций. Abstract A large audio-visual deepfake benchmark and standardized evaluation suite show that diverse composite-forgery training improves detection performance but does not reliably generalize to constituent manipulations, while modality masking and temporal misalignment increase detection errors.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Audio-visual deepfakes can spread misinformation and enable impersonation, undermining trust in digital media. However, existing datasets provide limited coverage of forgery operations and their combinations, constraining detector development and systematic evaluation. We introduce Mega-MMDF, containing 100,000 real and 1.2 million forged clips across 22 pipelines spanning modality-specific manipulations, stacked forgeries, and joint audio-video generation. To assess its training value and enable fair comparisons, we develop DeepfakeBench-MM, which standardizes training and evaluation across 8 datasets and 10 detectors. Training-based detectors achieve their highest mean AUC when trained on Mega-MMDF, with gains of up to 0.0784 over the best alternative training dataset for each detector. Experiments on data scale and pipeline coverage further support the value of large, diverse training data. Cross-pipeline evaluations reveal bidirectional and asymmetric generalization. Training on composite forgeries does not necessarily enable detection of their constituent operations in isolation. Our analysis of altered inputs shows that modality masking can increase false alarms and missed detections, and temporal misalignment can raise mean predicted forgery probabilities. Together, these resources provide a foundation for understanding and improving multimodal deepfake detection, with publicly available code and dataset access subject to application and usage terms.

"Abstract A large audio-visual deepfake benchmark and standardized evaluation suite show that diverse composite-forgery training improves detection performance but does not reliably generalize to constituent manipulations, while modality masking and temporal misalignment increase detection errors.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Audio-visual deepfakes can spread misinformation and enable impersonation, undermining trust in digital media. However, existing datasets provide limited coverage of forgery operations and their combinations, constraining detector development and systematic evaluation. We introduce Mega-MMDF, containing 100,000 real and 1.2 million forged clips across 22 pipelines spanning modality-specific manipulations, stacked forgeries, and joint audio-video generation. To assess its training value and enable fair comparisons, we develop DeepfakeBench-MM, which standardizes training and evaluation across 8 datasets and 10 detectors. Training-based detectors achieve their highest mean AUC when trained on Mega-MMDF, with gains of up to 0.0784 over the best alternative training dataset for each detector. Experiments on data scale and pipeline coverage further support the value of large, diverse training data. Cross-pipeline evaluations reveal bidirectional and asymmetric generalization. Training on composite forgeries does not necessarily enable detection of their constituent operations in isolation. Our analysis of altered inputs shows that modality masking can increase false alarms and missed detections, and temporal misalignment can raise mean predicted forgery probabilities. Together, these resources provide a foundation for understanding and improving multimodal deepfake detection, with publicly available code and dataset access subject to application and usage terms."

Abstract  Visual creation demands expressive imagery together with precise control over text, layout, and structure. We introduce Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation, an agentic framework that connects these capabilities through Pixel as Code. Raster images are represented by learned discrete visual tokens, while structured artifacts use explicit programs. A shared generation-and-rendering interface connects these representations to visual feedback across images, vector graphics, webpages, and documents. A Nemotron-native harness coordinates planning and raster candidate selection, with targeted repair for editable structured and hybrid artifacts. To support this interface, we develop Nemotron-Labs-Image-Tokenizer, the autoregressive raster generator Nemotron-Labs-Image-30B-A3B, and the structured-code model family Nemotron-Labs-VisionCode. On approximately 178K images spanning six visual domains, our tokenizer improves reconstruction PSNR from 26.08 to 29.57 dB over Emu3.5 IBQ. It also provides 7.13x faster encoding and 10.93x faster decoding at batch size one on A100. In standalone evaluation, one Nemotron-Labs-VisionCode variant surpasses Kimi K3.1 Pro on the aggregate across Chart2Code, MMSVG, Design2Code, and Image2Struct. At the system level, the Nemotron-native configuration raises GenEval2 Soft-TIFA GM from 31.71 to 52.56 using the same raster generator. With our best setting, Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation achieves performance competitive with GPT Image 2 and Nano Banana Pro across most benchmarks with available comparisons.

Abstract  Split-training privacy evaluations fail through distinct mechanisms. Evaluators can overlook an exposed gradient channel, align reconstructions against shifted labels, or deploy uncalibrated probes before testing a candidate defence, while calibrated attacks can also refute real defences. Separating these failure modes alters which claims survive. We conduct a retrospective evidentiary reanalysis across four split-LLM campaigns and two cross-cutting scope checks covering host key custody and variational mutual-information bounds, mapping every recorded claim to one of five admissibility states (breach, non-detection, undetermined coverage, invalid measurement, or out-of-scope inference). Across a 19-configuration exploration, only four of 24 required probes qualified, yet those four calibrated probes still established qualified privacy breaches in 13 configurations, while utility or learning-gain failures eliminated the remaining 6. In a seven-cell defence-redesign pilot, 2 quantised configurations failed utility gates, calibrated next-byte structure probes breached the 0.02 gate on all 7 cells (0.097 to 0.112), and qualified punctuation-property probes reached 0.79 to 0.89, yielding 4 failed and 3 undetermined composite privacy assessments (the latter from marginal null-band excursions on punctuation bindings). Negative evidence against both defence families remains intact. Scope checks further show that shared-key orthogonal wrappers protect passive network captures without hiding activations from a compromised restoring host, and why near-zero variational lower bounds leave worst-case leakage unconstrained. Rerunnable scripts verify 1,522 checks across 122 SHA-256-pinned artifacts while preserving each campaign's native seed intervals and retrospective boundaries.

Abstract A pretrained diffusion transformer is distilled into a weight-shared looped student that supports variable loop counts at inference and uses depth-wise ODE solvers and loop guidance to match teacher quality with fewer parameters and less compute.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Looped transformers reuse one block of shared weights along the depth of the network. Recent looped diffusion transformers are trained from scratch, which repeats the full pretraining cost although strong pretrained non-looped diffusion transformers exist. We propose Distill to Loop (Dooop), which distills a pretrained non-looped diffusion transformer into a looped student whose blocks are initialized from the teacher's. We cast distillation as a fixed-point iteration toward the teacher, in which the output after every loop is trained toward the teacher, and each loop is one step of an ordinary differential equation in depth. As a result, one model runs any number of loops at test time without retraining, and the higher-order ODE solvers of diffusion model sampling can be applied along the depth dimension for better results. Meanwhile, because a looped model produces an output after every loop, we also construct loop guidance between the outputs of different loop counts, which improves the generated samples at little extra compute. On SD3-medium, a looped student with about 37% of the teacher's parameters matches the teacher on average over GenEval, ImageReward, PickScore and HPSv2.1 with two to four loops, and with two loops it uses about 58% of the teacher's compute. And on a four-step SD3-medium, loop guidance raises the average of the four metrics from 88.2 to 99.2.
[25.11.2026 17:53] Response: {"desc": "Авторы предлагают метод Distill to Loop (Dooop), который дистиллирует предобученный диффузионный трансформер без циклов в looped-студента с общими весами блоков. Обучение строится как итерация с неподвижной точкой к выходу учителя, где каждый цикл рассматривается как шаг обыкновенного дифференциального уравнения по глубине сети. Такая модель работает с любым числом циклов на инференсе без переобучения, а для сэмплирования по глубине применяются ODE-солверы высокого порядка. Дополнительный loop guidance, основанный на разнице выходов при разном числе циклов, на SD3-medium с примерно 37% параметров учителя почти сохраняет качество и заметно повышает результат при четырёх шагах.", "emoji": "🔁", "title": "Один блок весов, любое число циклов: дистилляция диффузионного трансформера в looped-модель"}

Abstract GRAPE-Bench jointly evaluates video reasoning under visual degradation and question difficulty, exposing model weaknesses and enabling improvement and control.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Despite strong performance on standard benchmarks, video reasoning models remain vulnerable to visual degradation, raising concerns about their reliability in safety-critical scenarios. Existing benchmarks address this concern in part by simulating visual degradation or probing model reasoning with challenging questions, leaving open how well models handle naturally degraded video and whether they can make the simpler judgments that matter for safe driving. To this end, we introduce GRAPE-Bench, which jointly evaluates video reasoning across visual degradation conditions and question difficulty. It combines a synthetic set spanning twelve corruption types across daily-life and driving videos with real-world footage of driving at night or in rain, snow, or fog. Each video is paired with three progressively challenging questions, from fundamental judgments with potential safety implications to demanding reasoning stress tests. Across open-weight and frontier models, we observe ranking reversals between synthetic and real-world conditions and level-specific weaknesses obscured by overall accuracy. Synthetic-data fine-tuning improves Qwen3.5-9B's real-world accuracy from 76.7% to 81.1%, while clean-privileged on-policy distillation reaches 80.4% in a separate reasoning-enabled setting. Through preference steering, we further demonstrate continuous control over a frozen model's trade-off between safety-relevant judgments and challenging reasoning, with little change in overall accuracy. Together, GRAPE-Bench provides a unified testbed for evaluating, improving, and controlling video reasoning under degraded visual conditions.
[25.11.2026 17:53] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет GRAPE-Bench — новый бенчмарк для оценки video reasoning у LLM и мультимодальных моделей в условиях деградации изображения и разной сложности вопросов. Он сочетает синтетические искажения (двенадцать типов) на бытовых и дорожных видео с реальными записями вождения ночью, в дождь, снег и туман. Каждое видео сопровождается тремя вопросами возрастающей сложности, от базовых суждений, важных для безопасности, до сложных задач на рассуждение. Авторы показывают, что fine-tuning на синтетических данных и on-policy distillation повышают точность, а preference steering позволяет управлять компромиссом между безопасными суждениями и сложным рассуждением у замороженной модели.", "emoji": "🚗", "title": "Видеорассуждения ИИ проверяют в тумане и темноте, чтобы сделать вождение безопаснее"}

Abstract SpatialArena is an open benchmark that evaluates geometric models across five 3D perception tasks using uniform metrics and robust alignment, revealing that single-image depth is near saturation on public benchmarks but progress now depends on cross-frame and cross-view consistency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash 3D perception is shifting from task-specific pipelines to geometric foundation models: monocular depth networks, feed-forward 3D/4D reconstruction models, and vision--language models that predict geometry. However, their progress cannot be measured reliably. Published numbers are not comparable, because every paper samples frames, sets resolutions, and selects test splits differently. They are not robust because the alignment required for every up-to-scale prediction can outweigh the gap between models: fitting depth in the wrong space nearly triples its error, and standard trajectory alignment understates the error of failing methods by up to 2.6times. And they rarely cover long recordings, dynamic scenes, or aerial views. We present SpatialArena, an open arena that scores geometric models with the same metrics and rules, with robust and deterministic alignment. SpatialArena spans five tasks, from monocular depth to SLAM. It covers 23 public datasets with 418,929 images across indoor, outdoor, aerial, object-centric, and dynamic scenes, together with Spatial Arena, our own captures with accurate poses and depth. Evaluating 52 models, we find that single-image depth is close to saturation on public benchmarks but not on large, uncontrolled scenes, and that progress now comes from consistency across frames and views, where feed-forward 3D/4D models match or surpass monocular specialists and overtake video depth models. The benchmark, toolbox, and leaderboard will be publicly released.

Для честного сравнения используются единые метрики, правила выборки кадров и детерминированное робастное выравнивание, которое устраняет искажения при сравнении моделей с неизвестным масштабом. [25.11.2026 17:53] OpenRouter request. Model: anthropic/claude-haiku-5.5. Abstract Large language models exhibit a middle-heavy intrinsic-dimensionality profile in their feed-forward layers, and using this profile to guide width allocation improves pruning under a fixed parameter budget.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Modern large language models (LLMs) keep the feed-forward network (FFN) intermediate width identical across every layer, a convention inherited from the original Transformer. Yet mechanistic work suggests layers at different depths do qualitatively different work, raising a natural question: are their capacity demands also non-uniform, and if so, can a fixed parameter budget be allocated more wisely than uniformly? To probe this, we apply principal component analysis (PCA) to the input of each FFN down-projection and measure, for every layer, its intrinsic dimensionality as the smallest number of principal components needed to reconstruct the activation up to a fixed cosine similarity. Scanning 58 open-weight LLMs spanning 14 families and 70M to 72B parameters, we find a recurring middle-heavy profile in which middle layers carry higher intrinsic dimensionality than edge layers; the profile is robust to the calibration corpus, even across English and Chinese, and is largely preserved through post-training fine-tuning. As a direct test that this signal is structurally meaningful, we use it to guide width pruning in a knowledge-distilled student under a matched parameter budget: the PCA-guided allocation outperforms the uniform-width convention by +2.3 points on Qwen3-8B and +2.5 on Qwen2.5-3B, while lower-dispersion teachers show smaller gaps (+0.9 on Gemma-3-4B, +0.5 on LLaMA-3-8B), and a budget-matched inverted control does worst on all four teachers. Across these four teachers, the size of the gap tracks how unevenly the teacher's intrinsic dimensionality is distributed across layers (a single-number summary we call σ_{99}), and the advantage persists through downstream supervised fine-tuning. Code is available at https://github.com/naver-ai/lidf.
[25.11.2026 17:53] Response: {"desc": "Авторы показывают, что в FFN-слоях современных LLM внутренняя размерность активаций (измеряемая через PCA) распределена неравномерно: средние слои несут больше информации, чем крайние. Это свойство устойчиво к выбору калибровочного корпуса и сохраняется после fine-tuning. Использование этого профиля для распределения ширины FFN при pruning в knowledge distillation при том же бюджете параметров даёт прирост качества относительно равномерной ширины. Чем сильнее неравномерность у учителя, тем больше выигрыш.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Средние слои LLM нужнее крайних: распределяйте ширину FFN с умом"}

Abstract  Model alignment is the primary safety and security mechanism for enforcing refusal of malicious prompts in modern foundation models; yet, existing adversarial evaluations implicitly assume underlying hardware integrity. In this paper, we present FATCAT, the first physical fault-injection attacks capable of eliminating refusal in modern large language and vision-language models. Specifically, we present a non-invasive electromagnetic (EM) fault injection attack targeting GPU-accelerated Systems-on-Chip with physical access, requiring neither software execution privileges nor model-layer access. By leveraging an autonomous Radio Frequency (RF) side-channel trigger to synchronize EM faults to the prompt prefill phase, the attack corrupts safety-critical memory transactions across off-chip LPDDR5 DRAM and on-chip LPDDR physical interfaces without crashing the system while preserving model coherence. Evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano and AGX Thor running Llama 3.2 1B and Gemma 3 1B, localized physical faults reliably force models to fulfill restricted requests, demonstrating that model-level alignment provides no robust security guarantees without hardware-level fault tolerance. Finally, we demonstrate the physical-world consequences of this vulnerability on an active 6-DoF robotic manipulator controlled by a SmolVLA vision-language-action policy. Our attack silently corrupts the model forcing the arm into abrupt, hazardous lunges; in two instances the robotic arm attempted to pinch the paper author -- proving that hardware faults can directly translate into real physical harm.
