Атака использует неинвазивное электромагнитное воздействие на GPU-ускоренные SoC, синхронизированное через RF side-channel триггер с фазой prefill промпта, и повреждает критичные операции с памятью LPDDR5. Эксперименты на NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano и AGX Thor с моделями Llama 3.2 1B и Gemma 3 1B показывают, что выравнивание модели (alignment) не даёт надёжной защиты без аппаратной отказоустойчивости. Abstract InstructReplay preserves an instruction-tuned language model's general abilities during fact fine-tuning by keeping its responses close to the initial model's answers on general instructions, reducing benchmark decline while still learning new facts.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Fine-tuning an instruction-tuned language model on new facts often erodes its general abilities. We find that standard fine-tuning lowers instruction-following, reasoning and coding benchmarks by 35 points on average, and that these losses track a collapse in answer length: fine-tuned models at least halve their responses, and the further a response drifts from the initial model's length, the more often it fails. We propose InstructReplay: the initial model answers a set of general instructions once, and during fine-tuning the model is kept close to the initial model on these answers. Across three model families and two domains, InstructReplay reduces the average decline on five general benchmarks from 28.1 to 2.4 points and keeps answer length close to the initial model's, while learning the new facts as well as standard fine-tuning. It balances learning and retention better than seven adaptation methods in all six settings. Controlled comparisons attribute the gain to replaying the model's own answers to general instructions rather than to the form of the loss.

Abstract A reinforcement learning framework, RECAP, optimizes context compaction by aligning compressed-context behavior with original-history behavior, improving long-horizon agent performance.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Context compaction enables large language model agents to tackle long-horizon tasks within limited context budgets by replacing interaction histories with compressed summaries. However, learning effective compaction jointly with the agent policy remains challenging, as summaries affect task success over many subsequent interactions. We introduce RECAP, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework based on the principle that a summary should preserve the behavior induced by the original interaction history. We formalize this principle with a behavior distortion objective that measures the divergence between the agent's subsequent behavior under compressed and original contexts. We show that the gradient of the distortion objective decomposes into two complementary learning signals: distortion-based reward for summary generation and self-distillation for subsequent actions. Experiments on Qwen3.5-4B show that RECAP consistently outperforms the task-reward-only RL baselines on both environments, leading to an absolute improvement of 4.6% and 4.3% on BrowseComp-plus and SWE-bench Verified, respectively. Ablations further highlight the value of jointly supervising summary generation and post-compaction behavior for RECAP.

Abstract Foresight Forcing distills geometry-aware spatial transition queries from a frozen 3D foundation model to improve robotic manipulation action prediction without requiring future observations at deployment.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Robotic manipulation policies can improve action prediction by anticipating how objects move and how their spatial relationships change. World Action Models (WAMs) learn to predict future visual observations, but their training objectives typically leave the underlying 3D geometric transitions implicit. To alleviate this problem, we introduce Foresight Forcing (F^{2}), a WAM that learns a spatial transition representation from changes in a pretrained 3D feature space. During training, a frozen 3D foundation model extracts patch-wise feature differences from aligned current and future observations that provide dense supervision for geometry-aware feature transitions. We distill these targets into spatial transition queries, which are progressively refined from the current observation, language instruction, and proprioceptive state, and use them alongside current visual features to generate actions. At deployment, the policy predicts the queries without future observations or the 3D teacher. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin2.0 demonstrate strong manipulation performance and improved robustness, including 95.01% average success on RoboTwin2.0 Full. On six real-world manipulation tasks, F^{2} achieves a mean task score of 85.8%.

Эти цели дистиллируются в spatial transition queries, которые уточняются по текущему изображению, языковой инструкции и proprioceptive state. Abstract  Dense-embedding retrievers can fail adversarially: when a query is answered by a document A and reversed by a near-copy B that differs from A by a small meaning-changing edit (a "trap"), raw cosine over E5-Large-v2 places B in the top-10 for 82-100% of queries at a pool of 5,000. We study two things. First, a small learned projection W: R^1024 -> R^d (d in {11, 32}), trained by a deterministic recipe on a declared set of cases, that suppresses the trap: for the declared cases, HN@10 = 0.0000 (no trap in the top-10) in all twelve (domain, pool-size) settings across four independent domains (legal, medical, patent, financial) and pools up to 100,000, while the correct paraphrase is retained for 93-99% of cases, under exact nearest-neighbour search. Second, a boundary-conditioned accelerator that serves the projection at that scale: an acceleration of spectral graph retrieval (SpectRAG) that replaces a dense eigendecomposition with a boundary-value solve, holding HN@10 = 0.0000 at n = 100,000 in every domain and retrieving the ~300-query suite in 0.6-1.1 s (~2-4 ms per query on average). Because the lens's output is the stored representation (a few floats per document), a hardened, specialised index per domain or tenant is ~90x smaller and dimension-proportionally faster than the full embedding, making many small declared indexes a practical alternative to one large vector store. The claim is scoped to the declared cases on the measured pools, with no probabilistic bound; the correct-answer rate drops for a reworded query against an unchanged corpus (about 0.79, trap suppression still holding). A projection transfers only partly to other domains, which we report in full. The recipe, lenses, trap sets and evaluation harness are released under AGPL-3.0; the accelerator's implementation is a reserved commercial component, and every HN number is reproducible under exact search.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract  Dense-embedding retrievers can fail adversarially: when a query is answered by a document A and reversed by a near-copy B that differs from A by a small meaning-changing edit (a "trap"), raw cosine over E5-Large-v2 places B in the top-10 for 82-100% of queries at a pool of 5,000. We study two things. First, a small learned projection W: R^1024 -> R^d (d in {11, 32}), trained by a deterministic recipe on a declared set of cases, that suppresses the trap: for the declared cases, HN@10 = 0.0000 (no trap in the top-10) in all twelve (domain, pool-size) settings across four independent domains (legal, medical, patent, financial) and pools up to 100,000, while the correct paraphrase is retained for 93-99% of cases, under exact nearest-neighbour search. Second, a boundary-conditioned accelerator that serves the projection at that scale: an acceleration of spectral graph retrieval (SpectRAG) that replaces a dense eigendecomposition with a boundary-value solve, holding HN@10 = 0.0000 at n = 100,000 in every domain and retrieving the ~300-query suite in 0.6-1.1 s (~2-4 ms per query on average). Because the lens's output is the stored representation (a few floats per document), a hardened, specialised index per domain or tenant is ~90x smaller and dimension-proportionally faster than the full embedding, making many small declared indexes a practical alternative to one large vector store. The claim is scoped to the declared cases on the measured pools, with no probabilistic bound; the correct-answer rate drops for a reworded query against an unchanged corpus (about 0.79, trap suppression still holding). A projection transfers only partly to other domains, which we report in full. The recipe, lenses, trap sets and evaluation harness are released under AGPL-3.0; the accelerator's implementation is a reserved commercial component, and every HN number is reproducible under exact search."

[25.11.2026 17:54] OpenRouter request. Model: anthropic/claude-haiku-5.5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract ReFocus alternates global and local reinforcement learning with DAG-based progress rewards and adaptive practice windows to target task bottlenecks, improving long-horizon manipulation success over a π0.5 baseline.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Online reinforcement learning (RL) enables vision-language-action policies to improve through environmental interaction. However, in long-horizon manipulation, sparse success/failure feedback provides little information about where policies encounter bottlenecks. Moreover, repeated full-task rollouts also leave these bottlenecks weakly analyzed and insufficiently practiced. To this end, we introduce ReFocus, a progress-guided multi-round RL framework that alternates between Global and Local RL to identify task-specific bottlenecks and allocate additional practice to them. Specifically, during Global RL, we decompose each task into a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of subtasks. Then a DAG-based Progress Reward is leveraged for both policy training and bottleneck diagnosis. Based on the identified bottlenecks, ReFocus selects Adaptive Practice Windows, defining task segments from an entry stage to a target stage. It allocates additional interaction to these segments during Local RL while updating the entire policy. After that, it evaluates the policy to perform the full task for the next round of RL, forming an iteration between Global and Local RL. Experiments on LIBERO-Long and RoboCasa365 show that ReFocus achieves success rates of 97.8% and 51.3%, respectively, compared with 90.6% and 39.3% for the π_{0.5} baseline. Furthermore, we evaluate a real-world adaptation on three long-horizon tasks. On BreadBaker, ReFocus achieves 95% success, compared with 30% for the π_{0.5}. Project page: https://refocus-robotics.github.io.
[25.11.2026 17:54] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен ReFocus — многораундовый фреймворк обучения с подкреплением (RL) для vision-language-action моделей, который чередует глобальное и локальное обучение. Задачи декомпозируются в направленный ациклический граф (DAG) подзадач, а DAG-based progress reward используется и для обучения политики, и для диагностики узких мест. На основе выявленных узких мест выбираются adaptive practice windows, на которых дополнительно выделяется взаимодействие со средой. Эксперименты на LIBERO-Long и RoboCasa365 показывают рост успешности по сравнению с базовой моделью π0.5, а также подтверждены результаты в реальном мире.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Тренируй не всё подряд, а то, что мешает: точечное RL-обучение роботов"}

Abstract  We present OWE-Bench (OpenWorldEngineer-Bench), a benchmark of 26 long-horizon engineering tasks written by domain experts in software engineering, physical engineering, machine learning, and scientific computing. Tasks range from optimizing kernels and designing an aircraft, to identifying the faults responsible for an earthquake. Each task requires significant domain knowledge and engineering work, with models averaging hundreds of millions of tokens per task. Solutions are scored on practical measures such as speed, cost and accuracy, both on provided cases the agent could test against and on hidden cases that an engineer would expect from the task description. The best-performing frontier model so far, Claude Fable 5.2, scores 54.6% and averages 211 million tokens per task. We highlight two findings. First, when models are given more time, they use more test-time compute and achieve higher scores. Models also scale differently with test-time compute: GPT-6 Astra achieves higher scores at shorter time limits and at equal token use, while Claude Fable 5.2 uses more tokens at longer limits to achieve the highest overall score. Second, each model scores lower on hidden cases than on the provided cases, by an average of 18 points, which we call the deployment gap. Models also fail to predict the deployment gap, overestimating their performance on hidden test cases by an average of 21 points. We hope that OWE-Bench grounds progress toward agents whose engineering solutions perform as well in deployment as they do in testing.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract  We present OWE-Bench (OpenWorldEngineer-Bench), a benchmark of 26 long-horizon engineering tasks written by domain experts in software engineering, physical engineering, machine learning, and scientific computing. Tasks range from optimizing kernels and designing an aircraft, to identifying the faults responsible for an earthquake. Each task requires significant domain knowledge and engineering work, with models averaging hundreds of millions of tokens per task. Solutions are scored on practical measures such as speed, cost and accuracy, both on provided cases the agent could test against and on hidden cases that an engineer would expect from the task description. The best-performing frontier model so far, Claude Fable 5.2, scores 54.6% and averages 211 million tokens per task. We highlight two findings. First, when models are given more time, they use more test-time compute and achieve higher scores. Models also scale differently with test-time compute: GPT-6 Astra achieves higher scores at shorter time limits and at equal token use, while Claude Fable 5.2 uses more tokens at longer limits to achieve the highest overall score. Second, each model scores lower on hidden cases than on the provided cases, by an average of 18 points, which we call the deployment gap. Models also fail to predict the deployment gap, overestimating their performance on hidden test cases by an average of 21 points. We hope that OWE-Bench grounds progress toward agents whose engineering solutions perform as well in deployment as they do in testing."
